People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

And then I’m somewhere else before I even realize I stopped doing the thing I meant to do. It’s been frustrating, I guess.

I try not to make it sound like an excuse, but… yeah, it happens pretty often.\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably was, at least in school. I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible kid or anything, but in elementary school my teachers would move my seat a lot because I’d talk when I wasn’t supposed to. I also remember forgetting homework and rushing through worksheets, then making dumb mistakes. So… I guess compared to other kids, yeah, I was kind of more restless or hard to keep on task.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s been frustrating, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in a huge way, I don’t think, but I do notice I’m usually moving somehow. Like if I’m sitting talking with someone, my leg will be bouncing, or I’ll be clicking a pen, or messing with my keys without really noticing I’m doing it. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I’m, like, reckless or anything. I mean, sometimes I’ll say something before I’ve really thought it through, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. I try not to make bad decisions on purpose, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do that more than I’d like to admit. I don’t mean to be rude, but like with my mom, or even my counselor, they’ll be talking and an answer just kind of pops into my head, and I say it before they’re finished. Then I realize I cut them off and feel bad. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping your belongings organized?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try to keep things somewhat together, but it doesn’t always work out. It’s more like, if I have a bunch of stuff I need to do—like call my counselor, clean my room, send in an application—I kind of don’t know where to start. Then I’ll start one thing, switch to another, and end up with everything half-done. I don’t know if that counts as “organized,” but that happens a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a hard time waiting for your turn in line or when waiting to participate in an activity?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I can wait, I don’t like make a scene or anything. But I do get irritated pretty fast, like at the clinic or in a line at the store. I’ll keep shifting around, checking the time, kind of just wanting it to be over already. I try to keep it to myself, though. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're easily distracted and have trouble focusing on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I can focus sometimes, but a lot of the time I get pulled off track really easily. Like if I’m trying to work on an application or something, a car outside, my phone, or even just some random thought will kind of send me in a different direction before I realize I stopped doing the thing. I try not to make it sound like an excuse, but… yeah, it happens pretty often.\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably was, at least in school. I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible kid or anything, but in elementary school my teachers would move my seat a lot because I’d talk when I wasn’t supposed to. I also remember forgetting homework and rushing through worksheets, then making dumb mistakes. So… I guess compared to other kids, yeah, I was kind of more restless or hard to keep on task.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting really upset or angry for no reason?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d say “for no reason.” I mean, I can get frustrated, yeah, but I usually try to keep it in and not take it out on people. I don’t think I’m like exploding all the time or anything. Maybe I get irritated quicker than I should sometimes, but… I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these kinds of difficulties with focus and restlessness for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it does go back pretty far. Even in elementary school, teachers were moving my seat because I’d talk out of turn, and I’d forget homework or rush through worksheets and make careless mistakes. So… yeah, it’s not really a new thing, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or staying on task in multiple areas of your life, such as at work, at home, or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. It’s not like I can never focus, but if I’m trying to watch a repair video or read the manual for a tool, after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve just been kind of staring at it and none of it really went in. Then I have to back up or reread it. I don’t know if that counts, but it happens enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity caused you any significant problems in your relationships, work, or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has, at least with work stuff. I’m trying to move toward becoming an automotive technician, and I know I’m good with the hands-on part, or I think I could be. But I’ve missed training deadlines and had to redo applications because I didn’t get things finished right or on time. So it’s kind of embarrassing, because I feel like I should be further along by now. I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible kid or anything, but in elementary school my teachers would move my seat a lot because I’d talk when I wasn’t supposed to. I also remember forgetting homework and rushing through worksheets, then making dumb mistakes. So… I guess compared to other kids, yeah, I was kind of more restless or hard to keep on task.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting really upset or angry for no reason?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d say “for no reason.” I mean, I can get frustrated, yeah, but I usually try to keep it in and not take it out on people. I don’t think I’m like exploding all the time or anything. Maybe I get irritated quicker than I should sometimes, but… I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these kinds of difficulties with focus and restlessness for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it does go back pretty far. Even in elementary school, teachers were moving my seat because I’d talk out of turn, and I’d forget homework or rush through worksheets and make careless mistakes. So… yeah, it’s not really a new thing, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or staying on task in multiple areas of your life, such as at work, at home, or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. It’s not like I can never focus, but if I’m trying to watch a repair video or read the manual for a tool, after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve just been kind of staring at it and none of it really went in. Then I have to back up or reread it. I don’t know if that counts, but it happens enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity caused you any significant problems in your relationships, work, or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has, at least with work stuff. I’m trying to move toward becoming an automotive technician, and I know I’m good with the hands-on part, or I think I could be. And then I’m somewhere else before I even realize I stopped doing the thing I meant to do. It’s been frustrating, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in a huge way, I don’t think, but I do notice I’m usually moving somehow. Like if I’m sitting talking with someone, my leg will be bouncing, or I’ll be clicking a pen, or messing with my keys without really noticing I’m doing it. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I’m, like, reckless or anything. I mean, sometimes I’ll say something before I’ve really thought it through, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. I try not to make bad decisions on purpose, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do that more than I’d like to admit. I don’t mean to be rude, but like with my mom, or even my counselor, they’ll be talking and an answer just kind of pops into my head, and I say it before they’re finished. Then I realize I cut them off and feel bad. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping your belongings organized?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try to keep things somewhat together, but it doesn’t always work out. It’s more like, if I have a bunch of stuff I need to do—like call my counselor, clean my room, send in an application—I kind of don’t know where to start. Then I’ll start one thing, switch to another, and end up with everything half-done. I don’t know if that counts as “organized,” but that happens a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a hard time waiting for your turn in line or when waiting to participate in an activity?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I can wait, I don’t like make a scene or anything. But I do get irritated pretty fast, like at the clinic or in a line at the store. I’ll keep shifting around, checking the time, kind of just wanting it to be over already. I try to keep it to myself, though. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're easily distracted and have trouble focusing on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I can focus sometimes, but a lot of the time I get pulled off track really easily. Like if I’m trying to work on an application or something, a car outside, my phone, or even just some random thought will kind of send me in a different direction before I realize I stopped doing the thing. I try not to make it sound like an excuse, but… yeah, it happens pretty often.\n\nDoctor: Were you more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably was, at least in school. I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible kid or anything, but in elementary school my teachers would move my seat a lot because I’d talk when I wasn’t supposed to. I also remember forgetting homework and rushing through worksheets, then making dumb mistakes. So… I guess compared to other kids, yeah, I was kind of more restless or hard to keep on task.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions, like getting really upset or angry for no reason?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d say “for no reason.” I mean, I can get frustrated, yeah, but I usually try to keep it in and not take it out on people. I don’t think I’m like exploding all the time or anything. Maybe I get irritated quicker than I should sometimes, but… I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these kinds of difficulties with focus and restlessness for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it does go back pretty far. Even in elementary school, teachers were moving my seat because I’d talk out of turn, and I’d forget homework or rush through worksheets and make careless mistakes. So… yeah, it’s not really a new thing, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or staying on task in multiple areas of your life, such as at work, at home, or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. It’s not like I can never focus, but if I’m trying to watch a repair video or read the manual for a tool, after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve just been kind of staring at it and none of it really went in. Then I have to back up or reread it. I don’t know if that counts, but it happens enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity caused you any significant problems in your relationships, work, or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has, at least with work stuff. I’m trying to move toward becoming an automotive technician, and I know I’m good with the hands-on part, or I think I could be. But I’ve missed training deadlines and had to redo applications because I didn’t get things finished right or on time. So it’s kind of embarrassing, because I feel like I should be further along by now. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties affected your relationships with friends or family in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a bit. I don’t think it’s like, huge fights or anything, but I do catch myself cutting people off, especially my mom. And even with my counselor sometimes, which I feel bad about. Like they’ll be talking and an answer pops into my head, and I just say it before they’re finished.\n\nI’m not trying to be rude, but I can see how it probably comes across that way sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any of these difficulties, like trouble focusing or being restless, when you were a teenager?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, but it was there even before high school, honestly. In elementary school teachers would move my seat around because I’d talk when I wasn’t supposed to, and I’d forget homework sometimes.\n\nI also remember rushing through worksheets just to be done, and then I’d have a bunch of little careless mistakes. I guess at the time it was treated like I just needed to try harder or slow down, but… yeah, it was kind of a pattern. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties when you were very young, like in preschool?\n\nPatient: Preschool… I’m honestly not totally sure. I don’t remember that far back very clearly, and I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut by elementary school, yeah, teachers definitely commented. They’d move my seat around because I talked out of turn, and I’d forget homework sometimes. I also used to rush through worksheets just to get them done, and then I’d make careless little mistakes. So… maybe not preschool that I can say for sure, but pretty early on, yeah. Does that help?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity consistently throughout your childhood and adolescence?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was constantly causing problems or anything, but it was pretty consistent.\n\nLike even in elementary school, teachers were moving my seat because I’d talk out of turn, and I’d forget homework. I’d also rush through worksheets just to get them finished, and then there’d be careless mistakes. That kind of thing kept showing up as I got older too, just maybe in different ways.\n\nSo… yeah, I’d say it wasn’t just a one-time phase. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and impulsivity caused any problems in your romantic relationships?\n\nPatient: Um… romantic relationships, I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nI mean, I’m sure I’m not perfect in relationships, but I don’t know if I’d say those things caused major problems, at least not that I can clearly point to. Maybe I’d need to think about it more. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity in two or more settings, such as at home, in social situations, or at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I can say it hasn’t felt like it’s only in one place, but I don’t want to overstate it either.\n\nLike, I notice it in different situations sometimes, but I’m not sure what counts as “significant” or how to separate that from just being stressed or distracted. Could you maybe clarify what you mean by settings? Does that kind of answer it?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it’s been pretty consistent. I don’t really remember a time where it just completely went away.

Like, even back in elementary school, teachers were always moving my seat because I’d talk out of turn, or I’d forget homework. And I’d rush through worksheets just to be done, but then I’d make careless mistakes.
